It just feels like I'm completely disconnected from my own life.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, do you keep having unwanted memories or nightmares about it, or find yourself avoiding driving or anything that reminds you of it? Have you also been feeling down most of the day nearly every day, even aside from thinking about the event?\n\nPatient: Yes, I keep waking up at 2 AM from vivid nightmares of being unable to breathe in that car, and during the day I'll suddenly feel like I'm right back on that dark road clutching the wheel. To cope, I've been cutting back on travel and even avoided visiting my parents because the idea of being far from medical care feels unbearable. And yes, even when I'm not actively thinking about it, I feel completely flat, distant, and unable to connect to any joy in my life.\n\nDoctor: How long has it been since the highway scare, and have these nightmares, flashbacks, avoidance, and numb mood been happening for more than a month?\n\nPatient: The scare on the highway happened about two and a half weeks ago, so it hasn't been a full month yet. However, all of these symptoms have been happening continuously, every single day, since that night.\n\nDoctor: During the highway scare, did you believe you were about to die or be seriously injured, or was it mainly a very stressful event without that level of danger? Did anyone actually get hurt?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was absolutely convinced I was having a heart attack and would die alone on that highway before help could reach me. Thankfully, no one was physically injured, and the hospital later told me it was a severe stress reaction, but the terror of believing I was about to die in that moment felt completely real.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, have these symptoms started to get better at all, or are they staying just as strong and interfering with your daily life? Have you been able to function at work and in relationships, or is it causing major impairment?\n\nPatient: They haven't let up at all and have been completely continuous since the incident. It's causing major impairment; I've had to cancel trips, step back from key meetings, and let my COO handle public events. Even at home, I'm turning down family plans, which is really worrying my husband and frustrating my board."

Thankfully, no one was physically injured, and the hospital later told me it was a severe stress reaction, but the terror of believing I was about to die in that moment felt completely real.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, have these symptoms started to get better at all, or are they staying just as strong and interfering with your daily life? Have you been able to function at work and in relationships, or is it causing major impairment?\n\nPatient: They haven't let up at all and have been completely continuous since the incident. It's causing major impairment; I've had to cancel trips, step back from key meetings, and let my COO handle public events. Even at home, I'm turning down family plans, which is really worrying my husband and frustrating my board.\n\nDoctor: Since the symptoms began, have there been any periods of at least a few days when the nightmares, flashbacks, and avoidance clearly eased up, or have they stayed intense the whole time? Also, are you now past one month since the highway scare?\n\nPatient: No, there haven't been any breaks at all; the intensity has remained completely constant without a single day of relief. And to answer your second question, it has only been about three weeks since the incident, so it hasn't been a full month yet.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, have you felt detached from yourself or like the world around you seems unreal or dreamlike? Or does it mostly feel like fear, nightmares, and avoidance without that sense of unreality?\n\nPatient: While I do feel emotionally flat and disconnected—almost like I'm watching someone else live my life—the world itself doesn't feel dreamlike or unreal. Instead, I am just consumed by intense fear, constant physical anxiety, vivid nightmares, and avoiding any travel because I'm terrified of being far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: During the highway incident, was there an actual crash or another event that truly threatened your life or could have caused serious injury, or was the main danger the feeling that you were having a heart attack? Also, did any doctor later confirm a real medical emergency?\n\nPatient: No, there was no car crash or physical accident; the threat was entirely the terrifying feeling that I was having a fatal heart attack alone on that highway. The doctors at the hospital ran emergent tests and confirmed it wasn't a heart attack, but rather a severe stress reaction, leaving me with completely clean heart tests.\n\nDoctor: At the time on the highway, was there any actual external danger—like a crash, loss of control of the car, or another life-threatening event—or was the fear mainly that you were dying from what felt like a heart attack? This helps me tell whether the event itself meets the kind of trauma exposure we look for.\n\nPatient: There was no car crash, loss of control, or any other external accident; I managed to pull over safely on the shoulder. The terrifying, life-threatening fear was entirely internal, as the sudden chest tightness and inability to breathe made me absolutely certain I was having a fatal heart attack and would die alone on that dark highway before help could reach me.\n\nDoctor: Before this highway episode, had you ever had sudden panic attacks or intense episodes of chest tightness, shortness of breath, or fear of dying that came out of the blue? Or was this the first time anything like that ever happened?"

Even at home, I'm turning down family plans, which is really worrying my husband and frustrating my board.\n\nDoctor: Since the symptoms began, have there been any periods of at least a few days when the nightmares, flashbacks, and avoidance clearly eased up, or have they stayed intense the whole time? The doctors at the hospital ran emergent tests and confirmed it wasn't a heart attack, but rather a severe stress reaction, leaving me with completely clean heart tests.\n\nDoctor: At the time on the highway, was there any actual external danger—like a crash, loss of control of the car, or another life-threatening event—or was the fear mainly that you were dying from what felt like a heart attack? The terrifying, life-threatening fear was entirely internal, as the sudden chest tightness and inability to breathe made me absolutely certain I was having a fatal heart attack and would die alone on that dark highway before help could reach me.\n\nDoctor: Before this highway episode, had you ever had sudden panic attacks or intense episodes of chest tightness, shortness of breath, or fear of dying that came out of the blue? Before that night on the highway, I had absolutely no history of panic attacks, sudden chest tightness, or any fear of dying.\n\nDoctor: Since that first episode on the highway, have you had any other sudden panic attacks that came out of the blue, not triggered by reminders of the event? Did anyone actually get hurt?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was absolutely convinced I was having a heart attack and would die alone on that highway before help could reach me. Thankfully, no one was physically injured, and the hospital later told me it was a severe stress reaction, but the terror of believing I was about to die in that moment felt completely real.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, have these symptoms started to get better at all, or are they staying just as strong and interfering with your daily life? Have you been able to function at work and in relationships, or is it causing major impairment?\n\nPatient: They haven't let up at all and have been completely continuous since the incident. It's causing major impairment; I've had to cancel trips, step back from key meetings, and let my COO handle public events. Even at home, I'm turning down family plans, which is really worrying my husband and frustrating my board.\n\nDoctor: Since the symptoms began, have there been any periods of at least a few days when the nightmares, flashbacks, and avoidance clearly eased up, or have they stayed intense the whole time? Also, are you now past one month since the highway scare?\n\nPatient: No, there haven't been any breaks at all; the intensity has remained completely constant without a single day of relief. And to answer your second question, it has only been about three weeks since the incident, so it hasn't been a full month yet.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, have you felt detached from yourself or like the world around you seems unreal or dreamlike? Or does it mostly feel like fear, nightmares, and avoidance without that sense of unreality?\n\nPatient: While I do feel emotionally flat and disconnected—almost like I'm watching someone else live my life—the world itself doesn't feel dreamlike or unreal. Instead, I am just consumed by intense fear, constant physical anxiety, vivid nightmares, and avoiding any travel because I'm terrified of being far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: During the highway incident, was there an actual crash or another event that truly threatened your life or could have caused serious injury, or was the main danger the feeling that you were having a heart attack? Also, did any doctor later confirm a real medical emergency?\n\nPatient: No, there was no car crash or physical accident; the threat was entirely the terrifying feeling that I was having a fatal heart attack alone on that highway. The doctors at the hospital ran emergent tests and confirmed it wasn't a heart attack, but rather a severe stress reaction, leaving me with completely clean heart tests.\n\nDoctor: At the time on the highway, was there any actual external danger—like a crash, loss of control of the car, or another life-threatening event—or was the fear mainly that you were dying from what felt like a heart attack? This helps me tell whether the event itself meets the kind of trauma exposure we look for.\n\nPatient: There was no car crash, loss of control, or any other external accident; I managed to pull over safely on the shoulder. The terrifying, life-threatening fear was entirely internal, as the sudden chest tightness and inability to breathe made me absolutely certain I was having a fatal heart attack and would die alone on that dark highway before help could reach me.\n\nDoctor: Before this highway episode, had you ever had sudden panic attacks or intense episodes of chest tightness, shortness of breath, or fear of dying that came out of the blue? Or was this the first time anything like that ever happened?\n\nPatient: This was the very first time I have ever experienced anything like that. Before that night on the highway, I had absolutely no history of panic attacks, sudden chest tightness, or any fear of dying.\n\nDoctor: Since that first episode on the highway, have you had any other sudden panic attacks that came out of the blue, not triggered by reminders of the event? Or do the intense fear episodes only happen when something reminds you of that night or of being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: They aren't random, but they do strike unexpectedly during mundane moments like brushing my teeth or sitting in a board meeting, always tied directly to that night. I'll suddenly feel like I'm right back in that car clutching the steering wheel, or the panic is triggered by me constantly monitoring my body and obsessing over a tiny chest flutter or being too far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway episode, have you had repeated panic attacks that seem to come out of nowhere even when you are not reminded of that night or worried about your health? Or do they only happen when something triggers memories of the event or fear of being far from medical help?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Even when they seem to strike out of nowhere during daily tasks like brushing my teeth, they are actually intense flashbacks where I feel right back on that dark road, convinced I'm dying. They are also triggered by me constantly monitoring my own chest flutters or by the sheer dread of being far from a hospital, which has made me avoid traveling.

She pulled over and called 911, was rushed to a small hospital, and spent the night undergoing emergent evaluations in an unfamiliar setting before being told it was not a myocardial infarction but a severe stress reaction. In the following days she tried to resume her demanding CEO schedule, but found herself cutting back on travel, delegating public appearances, and struggling to maintain her usual early‑morning routines, which she relied on for balance; board members began asking if her health was compromising her leadership. When she realized she was going through the motions at work and avoiding planning an upcoming trip to visit her parents for Navratri because the idea of being far from medical care felt unbearable, she accepted her primary care physician’s recommendation for a psychiatric consultation.\n- Chief Complaint: “After that scare on the highway, it’s like a switch flipped—I’m going through my life and bolt out of the room or call 911 again even though I know I’m technically fine.\n- S071 (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): What scares me is that there are whole chunks of that day I just can’t piece satisfied feeling, but for the past few weeks everything—wins at work, my granddaughter’s video calls, my husband trying to celebrate my ‘clean’ heart tests—just feels flat and distant, like I’m watching someone else’s life and can’t connect to any of the good parts.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I find myself snapping at my team and at home over the smallest things—someone being five minutes late to a Zoom, my husband asking if I’ve taken my meds—and I go from zero to furious so fast that afterwards I’m almost shocked at the harsh, biting tone that came out of my mouth.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): I feel like my body never got the message that the emergency is over—whether I’m in the office, at home, or even in a restaurant, I’m constantly checking where the exits and nearest hospitals are, jumping at any sudden noise, and monitoring every flutter in my chest as if the next one might be the real heart attack.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last couple of weeks I’ve started canceling trips, cutting back on key meetings, and letting my COO handle public events because I don’t trust myself to cope, and even at home I keep turning down family plans, which is starting to worry my husband and frustrate my board.\n- duration: All of this has been going on continuously since that scare on the highway about three weeks ago and hasn’t really let up.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: After that scare on the highway a few weeks ago, it’s like a switch flipped. I’m going through my life and work, but I can’t feel any real joy or satisfaction in anything right now. It just feels like I'm completely disconnected from my own life.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, do you keep having unwanted memories or nightmares about it, or find yourself avoiding driving or anything that reminds you of it? Have you also been feeling down most of the day nearly every day, even aside from thinking about the event?\n\nPatient: Yes, I keep waking up at 2 AM from vivid nightmares of being unable to breathe in that car, and during the day I'll suddenly feel like I'm right back on that dark road clutching the wheel. To cope, I've been cutting back on travel and even avoided visiting my parents because the idea of being far from medical care feels unbearable. And yes, even when I'm not actively thinking about it, I feel completely flat, distant, and unable to connect to any joy in my life.\n\nDoctor: How long has it been since the highway scare, and have these nightmares, flashbacks, avoidance, and numb mood been happening for more than a month?\n\nPatient: The scare on the highway happened about two and a half weeks ago, so it hasn't been a full month yet. However, all of these symptoms have been happening continuously, every single day, since that night.\n\nDoctor: During the highway scare, did you believe you were about to die or be seriously injured, or was it mainly a very stressful event without that level of danger? Did anyone actually get hurt?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was absolutely convinced I was having a heart attack and would die alone on that highway before help could reach me. Thankfully, no one was physically injured, and the hospital later told me it was a severe stress reaction, but the terror of believing I was about to die in that moment felt completely real.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, have these symptoms started to get better at all, or are they staying just as strong and interfering with your daily life? Have you been able to function at work and in relationships, or is it causing major impairment?\n\nPatient: They haven't let up at all and have been completely continuous since the incident. It's causing major impairment; I've had to cancel trips, step back from key meetings, and let my COO handle public events. Even at home, I'm turning down family plans, which is really worrying my husband and frustrating my board.\n\nDoctor: Since the symptoms began, have there been any periods of at least a few days when the nightmares, flashbacks, and avoidance clearly eased up, or have they stayed intense the whole time? Also, are you now past one month since the highway scare?\n\nPatient: No, there haven't been any breaks at all; the intensity has remained completely constant without a single day of relief. And to answer your second question, it has only been about three weeks since the incident, so it hasn't been a full month yet.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, have you felt detached from yourself or like the world around you seems unreal or dreamlike? Or does it mostly feel like fear, nightmares, and avoidance without that sense of unreality?\n\nPatient: While I do feel emotionally flat and disconnected—almost like I'm watching someone else live my life—the world itself doesn't feel dreamlike or unreal. Instead, I am just consumed by intense fear, constant physical anxiety, vivid nightmares, and avoiding any travel because I'm terrified of being far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: During the highway incident, was there an actual crash or another event that truly threatened your life or could have caused serious injury, or was the main danger the feeling that you were having a heart attack? Also, did any doctor later confirm a real medical emergency?\n\nPatient: No, there was no car crash or physical accident; the threat was entirely the terrifying feeling that I was having a fatal heart attack alone on that highway. The doctors at the hospital ran emergent tests and confirmed it wasn't a heart attack, but rather a severe stress reaction, leaving me with completely clean heart tests.\n\nDoctor: At the time on the highway, was there any actual external danger—like a crash, loss of control of the car, or another life-threatening event—or was the fear mainly that you were dying from what felt like a heart attack? This helps me tell whether the event itself meets the kind of trauma exposure we look for.\n\nPatient: There was no car crash, loss of control, or any other external accident; I managed to pull over safely on the shoulder. The terrifying, life-threatening fear was entirely internal, as the sudden chest tightness and inability to breathe made me absolutely certain I was having a fatal heart attack and would die alone on that dark highway before help could reach me.\n\nDoctor: Before this highway episode, had you ever had sudden panic attacks or intense episodes of chest tightness, shortness of breath, or fear of dying that came out of the blue? Or was this the first time anything like that ever happened?\n\nPatient: This was the very first time I have ever experienced anything like that. Before that night on the highway, I had absolutely no history of panic attacks, sudden chest tightness, or any fear of dying.\n\nDoctor: Since that first episode on the highway, have you had any other sudden panic attacks that came out of the blue, not triggered by reminders of the event? Or do the intense fear episodes only happen when something reminds you of that night or of being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: They aren't random, but they do strike unexpectedly during mundane moments like brushing my teeth or sitting in a board meeting, always tied directly to that night. I'll suddenly feel like I'm right back in that car clutching the steering wheel, or the panic is triggered by me constantly monitoring my body and obsessing over a tiny chest flutter or being too far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway episode, have you had repeated panic attacks that seem to come out of nowhere even when you are not reminded of that night or worried about your health? Or do they only happen when something triggers memories of the event or fear of being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: Even when they seem to strike out of nowhere during daily tasks like brushing my teeth, they are actually intense flashbacks where I feel right back on that dark road, convinced I'm dying. They are also triggered by me constantly monitoring my own chest flutters or by the sheer dread of being far from a hospital, which has made me avoid traveling.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway episode, have you had any full panic attacks that truly came out of the blue, with no reminder of that night and no health-related worry beforehand? Or do they always happen in connection with memories of the event or fear about your body or being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: No, they are always connected to that night or my physical worries. Even when they seem to strike out of nowhere, it's actually a sudden flashback where I feel right back on that dark road clutching the wheel, or it's triggered by me constantly monitoring my chest flutters and worrying about being too far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: Since the hospital told you your heart was fine, do you still spend a lot of time worrying that you have a serious heart problem despite reassurance? Or is your fear mainly about reliving that highway episode rather than believing you’re currently medically ill?"

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: After that scare on the highway a few weeks ago, it’s like a switch flipped. I’m going through my life and work, but I can’t feel any real joy or satisfaction in anything right now. It just feels like I'm completely disconnected from my own life.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, do you keep having unwanted memories or nightmares about it, or find yourself avoiding driving or anything that reminds you of it? Have you also been feeling down most of the day nearly every day, even aside from thinking about the event?\n\nPatient: Yes, I keep waking up at 2 AM from vivid nightmares of being unable to breathe in that car, and during the day I'll suddenly feel like I'm right back on that dark road clutching the wheel. To cope, I've been cutting back on travel and even avoided visiting my parents because the idea of being far from medical care feels unbearable. And yes, even when I'm not actively thinking about it, I feel completely flat, distant, and unable to connect to any joy in my life.\n\nDoctor: How long has it been since the highway scare, and have these nightmares, flashbacks, avoidance, and numb mood been happening for more than a month?\n\nPatient: The scare on the highway happened about two and a half weeks ago, so it hasn't been a full month yet. However, all of these symptoms have been happening continuously, every single day, since that night.\n\nDoctor: During the highway scare, did you believe you were about to die or be seriously injured, or was it mainly a very stressful event without that level of danger? Did anyone actually get hurt?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was absolutely convinced I was having a heart attack and would die alone on that highway before help could reach me. Thankfully, no one was physically injured, and the hospital later told me it was a severe stress reaction, but the terror of believing I was about to die in that moment felt completely real.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, have these symptoms started to get better at all, or are they staying just as strong and interfering with your daily life? Have you been able to function at work and in relationships, or is it causing major impairment?\n\nPatient: They haven't let up at all and have been completely continuous since the incident. It's causing major impairment; I've had to cancel trips, step back from key meetings, and let my COO handle public events. Even at home, I'm turning down family plans, which is really worrying my husband and frustrating my board.\n\nDoctor: Since the symptoms began, have there been any periods of at least a few days when the nightmares, flashbacks, and avoidance clearly eased up, or have they stayed intense the whole time? Also, are you now past one month since the highway scare?\n\nPatient: No, there haven't been any breaks at all; the intensity has remained completely constant without a single day of relief. And to answer your second question, it has only been about three weeks since the incident, so it hasn't been a full month yet.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, have you felt detached from yourself or like the world around you seems unreal or dreamlike? Or does it mostly feel like fear, nightmares, and avoidance without that sense of unreality?\n\nPatient: While I do feel emotionally flat and disconnected—almost like I'm watching someone else live my life—the world itself doesn't feel dreamlike or unreal. Instead, I am just consumed by intense fear, constant physical anxiety, vivid nightmares, and avoiding any travel because I'm terrified of being far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: During the highway incident, was there an actual crash or another event that truly threatened your life or could have caused serious injury, or was the main danger the feeling that you were having a heart attack? Also, did any doctor later confirm a real medical emergency?\n\nPatient: No, there was no car crash or physical accident; the threat was entirely the terrifying feeling that I was having a fatal heart attack alone on that highway. The doctors at the hospital ran emergent tests and confirmed it wasn't a heart attack, but rather a severe stress reaction, leaving me with completely clean heart tests.\n\nDoctor: At the time on the highway, was there any actual external danger—like a crash, loss of control of the car, or another life-threatening event—or was the fear mainly that you were dying from what felt like a heart attack? This helps me tell whether the event itself meets the kind of trauma exposure we look for.\n\nPatient: There was no car crash, loss of control, or any other external accident; I managed to pull over safely on the shoulder. The terrifying, life-threatening fear was entirely internal, as the sudden chest tightness and inability to breathe made me absolutely certain I was having a fatal heart attack and would die alone on that dark highway before help could reach me.\n\nDoctor: Before this highway episode, had you ever had sudden panic attacks or intense episodes of chest tightness, shortness of breath, or fear of dying that came out of the blue? Or was this the first time anything like that ever happened?\n\nPatient: This was the very first time I have ever experienced anything like that. Before that night on the highway, I had absolutely no history of panic attacks, sudden chest tightness, or any fear of dying.\n\nDoctor: Since that first episode on the highway, have you had any other sudden panic attacks that came out of the blue, not triggered by reminders of the event? Or do the intense fear episodes only happen when something reminds you of that night or of being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: They aren't random, but they do strike unexpectedly during mundane moments like brushing my teeth or sitting in a board meeting, always tied directly to that night. I'll suddenly feel like I'm right back in that car clutching the steering wheel, or the panic is triggered by me constantly monitoring my body and obsessing over a tiny chest flutter or being too far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway episode, have you had repeated panic attacks that seem to come out of nowhere even when you are not reminded of that night or worried about your health? Or do they only happen when something triggers memories of the event or fear of being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: Even when they seem to strike out of nowhere during daily tasks like brushing my teeth, they are actually intense flashbacks where I feel right back on that dark road, convinced I'm dying. They are also triggered by me constantly monitoring my own chest flutters or by the sheer dread of being far from a hospital, which has made me avoid traveling.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway episode, have you had any full panic attacks that truly came out of the blue, with no reminder of that night and no health-related worry beforehand? Or do they always happen in connection with memories of the event or fear about your body or being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: No, they are always connected to that night or my physical worries. Even when they seem to strike out of nowhere, it's actually a sudden flashback where I feel right back on that dark road clutching the wheel, or it's triggered by me constantly monitoring my chest flutters and worrying about being too far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: Since the hospital told you your heart was fine, do you still spend a lot of time worrying that you have a serious heart problem despite reassurance? Or is your fear mainly about reliving that highway episode rather than believing you’re currently medically ill?\n\nPatient: It is actually a combination of both. Despite the hospital's reassurance, I am constantly on edge, monitoring every flutter in my chest with the fear that the next one will be a real heart attack. At the same time, I frequently feel pulled back onto that dark highway, reliving the panic of that night as if it's happening all over again.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, what takes up more of your mind: fear that you currently have a serious heart condition despite normal tests, or fear and memories of reliving that highway episode? Which one is driving your avoidance and distress the most right now?"

Have you also been feeling down most of the day nearly every day, even aside from thinking about the event?\n\nPatient: Yes, I keep waking up at 2 AM from vivid nightmares of being unable to breathe in that car, and during the day I'll suddenly feel like I'm right back on that dark road clutching the wheel. To cope, I've been cutting back on travel and even avoided visiting my parents because the idea of being far from medical care feels unbearable. And yes, even when I'm not actively thinking about it, I feel completely flat, distant, and unable to connect to any joy in my life.\n\nDoctor: How long has it been since the highway scare, and have these nightmares, flashbacks, avoidance, and numb mood been happening for more than a month?\n\nPatient: The scare on the highway happened about two and a half weeks ago, so it hasn't been a full month yet. However, all of these symptoms have been happening continuously, every single day, since that night.\n\nDoctor: During the highway scare, did you believe you were about to die or be seriously injured, or was it mainly a very stressful event without that level of danger? Did anyone actually get hurt?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was absolutely convinced I was having a heart attack and would die alone on that highway before help could reach me. Thankfully, no one was physically injured, and the hospital later told me it was a severe stress reaction, but the terror of believing I was about to die in that moment felt completely real.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, have these symptoms started to get better at all, or are they staying just as strong and interfering with your daily life? Have you been able to function at work and in relationships, or is it causing major impairment?\n\nPatient: They haven't let up at all and have been completely continuous since the incident. It's causing major impairment; I've had to cancel trips, step back from key meetings, and let my COO handle public events. Even at home, I'm turning down family plans, which is really worrying my husband and frustrating my board.\n\nDoctor: Since the symptoms began, have there been any periods of at least a few days when the nightmares, flashbacks, and avoidance clearly eased up, or have they stayed intense the whole time? Also, are you now past one month since the highway scare?\n\nPatient: No, there haven't been any breaks at all; the intensity has remained completely constant without a single day of relief. And to answer your second question, it has only been about three weeks since the incident, so it hasn't been a full month yet.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, have you felt detached from yourself or like the world around you seems unreal or dreamlike? Or does it mostly feel like fear, nightmares, and avoidance without that sense of unreality?\n\nPatient: While I do feel emotionally flat and disconnected—almost like I'm watching someone else live my life—the world itself doesn't feel dreamlike or unreal. Instead, I am just consumed by intense fear, constant physical anxiety, vivid nightmares, and avoiding any travel because I'm terrified of being far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: During the highway incident, was there an actual crash or another event that truly threatened your life or could have caused serious injury, or was the main danger the feeling that you were having a heart attack? Also, did any doctor later confirm a real medical emergency?\n\nPatient: No, there was no car crash or physical accident; the threat was entirely the terrifying feeling that I was having a fatal heart attack alone on that highway. The doctors at the hospital ran emergent tests and confirmed it wasn't a heart attack, but rather a severe stress reaction, leaving me with completely clean heart tests.\n\nDoctor: At the time on the highway, was there any actual external danger—like a crash, loss of control of the car, or another life-threatening event—or was the fear mainly that you were dying from what felt like a heart attack? This helps me tell whether the event itself meets the kind of trauma exposure we look for.\n\nPatient: There was no car crash, loss of control, or any other external accident; I managed to pull over safely on the shoulder. The terrifying, life-threatening fear was entirely internal, as the sudden chest tightness and inability to breathe made me absolutely certain I was having a fatal heart attack and would die alone on that dark highway before help could reach me.\n\nDoctor: Before this highway episode, had you ever had sudden panic attacks or intense episodes of chest tightness, shortness of breath, or fear of dying that came out of the blue? Or was this the first time anything like that ever happened?\n\nPatient: This was the very first time I have ever experienced anything like that. Before that night on the highway, I had absolutely no history of panic attacks, sudden chest tightness, or any fear of dying.\n\nDoctor: Since that first episode on the highway, have you had any other sudden panic attacks that came out of the blue, not triggered by reminders of the event? Or do the intense fear episodes only happen when something reminds you of that night or of being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: They aren't random, but they do strike unexpectedly during mundane moments like brushing my teeth or sitting in a board meeting, always tied directly to that night. I'll suddenly feel like I'm right back in that car clutching the steering wheel, or the panic is triggered by me constantly monitoring my body and obsessing over a tiny chest flutter or being too far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway episode, have you had repeated panic attacks that seem to come out of nowhere even when you are not reminded of that night or worried about your health? Or do they only happen when something triggers memories of the event or fear of being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: Even when they seem to strike out of nowhere during daily tasks like brushing my teeth, they are actually intense flashbacks where I feel right back on that dark road, convinced I'm dying. They are also triggered by me constantly monitoring my own chest flutters or by the sheer dread of being far from a hospital, which has made me avoid traveling.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway episode, have you had any full panic attacks that truly came out of the blue, with no reminder of that night and no health-related worry beforehand? Or do they always happen in connection with memories of the event or fear about your body or being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: No, they are always connected to that night or my physical worries. Even when they seem to strike out of nowhere, it's actually a sudden flashback where I feel right back on that dark road clutching the wheel, or it's triggered by me constantly monitoring my chest flutters and worrying about being too far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: Since the hospital told you your heart was fine, do you still spend a lot of time worrying that you have a serious heart problem despite reassurance? Or is your fear mainly about reliving that highway episode rather than believing you’re currently medically ill?\n\nPatient: It is actually a combination of both. Despite the hospital's reassurance, I am constantly on edge, monitoring every flutter in my chest with the fear that the next one will be a real heart attack. At the same time, I frequently feel pulled back onto that dark highway, reliving the panic of that night as if it's happening all over again.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, what takes up more of your mind: fear that you currently have a serious heart condition despite normal tests, or fear and memories of reliving that highway episode? Which one is driving your avoidance and distress the most right now?\n\nPatient: Both are incredibly overwhelming, but they affect me differently: the sudden intrusive memories hit out of nowhere and make me feel like I'm actively dying on that road again, while the background fear keeps me hyper-focused on my chest and constantly checking for nearby hospitals. Ultimately, it's the dread of being far from medical care if another episode happens that drives my avoidance the most, like being completely unable to plan the trip to visit my parents."
  }
]

It's causing major impairment; I've had to cancel trips, step back from key meetings, and let my COO handle public events. Even at home, I'm turning down family plans, which is really worrying my husband and frustrating my board.\n\nDoctor: Since the symptoms began, have there been any periods of at least a few days when the nightmares, flashbacks, and avoidance clearly eased up, or have they stayed intense the whole time? Also, are you now past one month since the highway scare?\n\nPatient: No, there haven't been any breaks at all; the intensity has remained completely constant without a single day of relief. And to answer your second question, it has only been about three weeks since the incident, so it hasn't been a full month yet.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, have you felt detached from yourself or like the world around you seems unreal or dreamlike? Or does it mostly feel like fear, nightmares, and avoidance without that sense of unreality?\n\nPatient: While I do feel emotionally flat and disconnected—almost like I'm watching someone else live my life—the world itself doesn't feel dreamlike or unreal. Instead, I am just consumed by intense fear, constant physical anxiety, vivid nightmares, and avoiding any travel because I'm terrified of being far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: During the highway incident, was there an actual crash or another event that truly threatened your life or could have caused serious injury, or was the main danger the feeling that you were having a heart attack? Also, did any doctor later confirm a real medical emergency?\n\nPatient: No, there was no car crash or physical accident; the threat was entirely the terrifying feeling that I was having a fatal heart attack alone on that highway. The doctors at the hospital ran emergent tests and confirmed it wasn't a heart attack, but rather a severe stress reaction, leaving me with completely clean heart tests.\n\nDoctor: At the time on the highway, was there any actual external danger—like a crash, loss of control of the car, or another life-threatening event—or was the fear mainly that you were dying from what felt like a heart attack? This helps me tell whether the event itself meets the kind of trauma exposure we look for.\n\nPatient: There was no car crash, loss of control, or any other external accident; I managed to pull over safely on the shoulder. The terrifying, life-threatening fear was entirely internal, as the sudden chest tightness and inability to breathe made me absolutely certain I was having a fatal heart attack and would die alone on that dark highway before help could reach me.\n\nDoctor: Before this highway episode, had you ever had sudden panic attacks or intense episodes of chest tightness, shortness of breath, or fear of dying that came out of the blue? Or was this the first time anything like that ever happened?\n\nPatient: This was the very first time I have ever experienced anything like that. Before that night on the highway, I had absolutely no history of panic attacks, sudden chest tightness, or any fear of dying.\n\nDoctor: Since that first episode on the highway, have you had any other sudden panic attacks that came out of the blue, not triggered by reminders of the event? Or do the intense fear episodes only happen when something reminds you of that night or of being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: They aren't random, but they do strike unexpectedly during mundane moments like brushing my teeth or sitting in a board meeting, always tied directly to that night. I'll suddenly feel like I'm right back in that car clutching the steering wheel, or the panic is triggered by me constantly monitoring my body and obsessing over a tiny chest flutter or being too far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway episode, have you had repeated panic attacks that seem to come out of nowhere even when you are not reminded of that night or worried about your health? Or do they only happen when something triggers memories of the event or fear of being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: Even when they seem to strike out of nowhere during daily tasks like brushing my teeth, they are actually intense flashbacks where I feel right back on that dark road, convinced I'm dying. They are also triggered by me constantly monitoring my own chest flutters or by the sheer dread of being far from a hospital, which has made me avoid traveling.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway episode, have you had any full panic attacks that truly came out of the blue, with no reminder of that night and no health-related worry beforehand? Or do they always happen in connection with memories of the event or fear about your body or being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: No, they are always connected to that night or my physical worries. Even when they seem to strike out of nowhere, it's actually a sudden flashback where I feel right back on that dark road clutching the wheel, or it's triggered by me constantly monitoring my chest flutters and worrying about being too far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: Since the hospital told you your heart was fine, do you still spend a lot of time worrying that you have a serious heart problem despite reassurance? Or is your fear mainly about reliving that highway episode rather than believing you’re currently medically ill?\n\nPatient: It is actually a combination of both. Despite the hospital's reassurance, I am constantly on edge, monitoring every flutter in my chest with the fear that the next one will be a real heart attack. At the same time, I frequently feel pulled back onto that dark highway, reliving the panic of that night as if it's happening all over again.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, what takes up more of your mind: fear that you currently have a serious heart condition despite normal tests, or fear and memories of reliving that highway episode? Which one is driving your avoidance and distress the most right now?\n\nPatient: Both are incredibly overwhelming, but they affect me differently: the sudden intrusive memories hit out of nowhere and make me feel like I'm actively dying on that road again, while the background fear keeps me hyper-focused on my chest and constantly checking for nearby hospitals. Ultimately, it's the dread of being far from medical care if another episode happens that drives my avoidance the most, like being completely unable to plan the trip to visit my parents.\n\nDoctor: If you were close to a hospital and felt confident help was immediately available, would you still avoid travel or public places because you might panic or be unable to escape? Or would the avoidance mostly go away?\n\nPatient: If I knew a hospital was immediately available, it would make a huge difference and ease most of my avoidance. My main fear isn't being in public, but having another 'heart attack' and dying on the side of the road before help can reach me, which is exactly why I've been avoiding the trip to see my parents. While I am still incredibly jumpy, the avoidance is driven by that fear of being far from medical care rather than just being out in public."

Also, are you now past one month since the highway scare?\n\nPatient: No, there haven't been any breaks at all; the intensity has remained completely constant without a single day of relief. And to answer your second question, it has only been about three weeks since the incident, so it hasn't been a full month yet.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, have you felt detached from yourself or like the world around you seems unreal or dreamlike? Or does it mostly feel like fear, nightmares, and avoidance without that sense of unreality?\n\nPatient: While I do feel emotionally flat and disconnected—almost like I'm watching someone else live my life—the world itself doesn't feel dreamlike or unreal. Instead, I am just consumed by intense fear, constant physical anxiety, vivid nightmares, and avoiding any travel because I'm terrified of being far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: During the highway incident, was there an actual crash or another event that truly threatened your life or could have caused serious injury, or was the main danger the feeling that you were having a heart attack? Also, did any doctor later confirm a real medical emergency?\n\nPatient: No, there was no car crash or physical accident; the threat was entirely the terrifying feeling that I was having a fatal heart attack alone on that highway. The doctors at the hospital ran emergent tests and confirmed it wasn't a heart attack, but rather a severe stress reaction, leaving me with completely clean heart tests.\n\nDoctor: At the time on the highway, was there any actual external danger—like a crash, loss of control of the car, or another life-threatening event—or was the fear mainly that you were dying from what felt like a heart attack? This helps me tell whether the event itself meets the kind of trauma exposure we look for.\n\nPatient: There was no car crash, loss of control, or any other external accident; I managed to pull over safely on the shoulder. The terrifying, life-threatening fear was entirely internal, as the sudden chest tightness and inability to breathe made me absolutely certain I was having a fatal heart attack and would die alone on that dark highway before help could reach me.\n\nDoctor: Before this highway episode, had you ever had sudden panic attacks or intense episodes of chest tightness, shortness of breath, or fear of dying that came out of the blue? Or was this the first time anything like that ever happened?\n\nPatient: This was the very first time I have ever experienced anything like that. Before that night on the highway, I had absolutely no history of panic attacks, sudden chest tightness, or any fear of dying.\n\nDoctor: Since that first episode on the highway, have you had any other sudden panic attacks that came out of the blue, not triggered by reminders of the event? Or do the intense fear episodes only happen when something reminds you of that night or of being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: They aren't random, but they do strike unexpectedly during mundane moments like brushing my teeth or sitting in a board meeting, always tied directly to that night. I'll suddenly feel like I'm right back in that car clutching the steering wheel, or the panic is triggered by me constantly monitoring my body and obsessing over a tiny chest flutter or being too far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway episode, have you had repeated panic attacks that seem to come out of nowhere even when you are not reminded of that night or worried about your health? Or do they only happen when something triggers memories of the event or fear of being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: Even when they seem to strike out of nowhere during daily tasks like brushing my teeth, they are actually intense flashbacks where I feel right back on that dark road, convinced I'm dying. They are also triggered by me constantly monitoring my own chest flutters or by the sheer dread of being far from a hospital, which has made me avoid traveling.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway episode, have you had any full panic attacks that truly came out of the blue, with no reminder of that night and no health-related worry beforehand? Or do they always happen in connection with memories of the event or fear about your body or being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: No, they are always connected to that night or my physical worries. Even when they seem to strike out of nowhere, it's actually a sudden flashback where I feel right back on that dark road clutching the wheel, or it's triggered by me constantly monitoring my chest flutters and worrying about being too far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: Since the hospital told you your heart was fine, do you still spend a lot of time worrying that you have a serious heart problem despite reassurance? Or is your fear mainly about reliving that highway episode rather than believing you’re currently medically ill?\n\nPatient: It is actually a combination of both. Despite the hospital's reassurance, I am constantly on edge, monitoring every flutter in my chest with the fear that the next one will be a real heart attack. At the same time, I frequently feel pulled back onto that dark highway, reliving the panic of that night as if it's happening all over again.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, what takes up more of your mind: fear that you currently have a serious heart condition despite normal tests, or fear and memories of reliving that highway episode? Which one is driving your avoidance and distress the most right now?\n\nPatient: Both are incredibly overwhelming, but they affect me differently: the sudden intrusive memories hit out of nowhere and make me feel like I'm actively dying on that road again, while the background fear keeps me hyper-focused on my chest and constantly checking for nearby hospitals. Ultimately, it's the dread of being far from medical care if another episode happens that drives my avoidance the most, like being completely unable to plan the trip to visit my parents.\n\nDoctor: If you were close to a hospital and felt confident help was immediately available, would you still avoid travel or public places because you might panic or be unable to escape? Or would the avoidance mostly go away?\n\nPatient: If I knew a hospital was immediately available, it would make a huge difference and ease most of my avoidance. My main fear isn't being in public, but having another 'heart attack' and dying on the side of the road before help can reach me, which is exactly why I've been avoiding the trip to see my parents. While I am still incredibly jumpy, the avoidance is driven by that fear of being far from medical care rather than just being out in public.\n\nDoctor: Since the hospital said your heart was healthy, have you been repeatedly checking your body, seeking reassurance, or looking up heart symptoms because you still believe you may have a serious illness? Or is the fear mainly about having another episode like that night rather than believing you currently have heart disease?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I am constantly monitoring every single flutter in my chest and keeping track of where the nearest hospitals and exits are. It’s not really about researching heart diseases, but rather that my body feels stuck in emergency mode. I am just terrified that the next flutter will be a real heart attack and I'll be trapped without help, just like that night on the highway.

Even at home, I'm turning down family plans, which is really worrying my husband and frustrating my board.\n\nDoctor: Since the symptoms began, have there been any periods of at least a few days when the nightmares, flashbacks, and avoidance clearly eased up, or have they stayed intense the whole time? And to answer your second question, it has only been about three weeks since the incident, so it hasn't been a full month yet.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway scare, have you felt detached from yourself or like the world around you seems unreal or dreamlike? The terrifying, life-threatening fear was entirely internal, as the sudden chest tightness and inability to breathe made me absolutely certain I was having a fatal heart attack and would die alone on that dark highway before help could reach me.\n\nDoctor: Before this highway episode, had you ever had sudden panic attacks or intense episodes of chest tightness, shortness of breath, or fear of dying that came out of the blue? I'll suddenly feel like I'm right back in that car clutching the steering wheel, or the panic is triggered by me constantly monitoring my body and obsessing over a tiny chest flutter or being too far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway episode, have you had repeated panic attacks that seem to come out of nowhere even when you are not reminded of that night or worried about your health? Or do they only happen when something triggers memories of the event or fear of being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: Even when they seem to strike out of nowhere during daily tasks like brushing my teeth, they are actually intense flashbacks where I feel right back on that dark road, convinced I'm dying. They are also triggered by me constantly monitoring my own chest flutters or by the sheer dread of being far from a hospital, which has made me avoid traveling.\n\nDoctor: Since the highway episode, have you had any full panic attacks that truly came out of the blue, with no reminder of that night and no health-related worry beforehand? Or do they always happen in connection with memories of the event or fear about your body or being far from medical help?\n\nPatient: No, they are always connected to that night or my physical worries. Even when they seem to strike out of nowhere, it's actually a sudden flashback where I feel right back on that dark road clutching the wheel, or it's triggered by me constantly monitoring my chest flutters and worrying about being too far from a hospital.\n\nDoctor: Since the hospital told you your heart was fine, do you still spend a lot of time worrying that you have a serious heart problem despite reassurance? Or is your fear mainly about reliving that highway episode rather than believing you’re currently medically ill?\n\nPatient: It is actually a combination of both. Despite the hospital's reassurance, I am constantly on edge, monitoring every flutter in my chest with the fear that the next one will be a real heart attack. At the same time, I frequently feel pulled back onto that dark highway, reliving the panic of that night as if it's happening all over again.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, what takes up more of your mind: fear that you currently have a serious heart condition despite normal tests, or fear and memories of reliving that highway episode? Which one is driving your avoidance and distress the most right now?\n\nPatient: Both are incredibly overwhelming, but they affect me differently: the sudden intrusive memories hit out of nowhere and make me feel like I'm actively dying on that road again, while the background fear keeps me hyper-focused on my chest and constantly checking for nearby hospitals. Ultimately, it's the dread of being far from medical care if another episode happens that drives my avoidance the most, like being completely unable to plan the trip to visit my parents.\n\nDoctor: If you were close to a hospital and felt confident help was immediately available, would you still avoid travel or public places because you might panic or be unable to escape? Or would the avoidance mostly go away?\n\nPatient: If I knew a hospital was immediately available, it would make a huge difference and ease most of my avoidance. My main fear isn't being in public, but having another 'heart attack' and dying on the side of the road before help can reach me, which is exactly why I've been avoiding the trip to see my parents. While I am still incredibly jumpy, the avoidance is driven by that fear of being far from medical care rather than just being out in public.\n\nDoctor: Since the hospital said your heart was healthy, have you been repeatedly checking your body, seeking reassurance, or looking up heart symptoms because you still believe you may have a serious illness? Or is the fear mainly about having another episode like that night rather than believing you currently have heart disease?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am constantly monitoring every single flutter in my chest and keeping track of where the nearest hospitals and exits are. It’s not really about researching heart diseases, but rather that my body feels stuck in emergency mode. I am just terrified that the next flutter will be a real heart attack and I'll be trapped without help, just like that night on the highway.\n\nDoctor: If your doctor repeated the tests today and again showed your heart is healthy, would your fear and avoidance mostly go away, or would you still keep worrying and checking because you might have a serious illness? This helps me tell whether the main problem is ongoing illness fear or reliving that highway episode."

Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
